### Thinking

 I'm grasping the core mechanics now: the separation fraction tells me how much of the divergence comes from sign versus magnitude, and the agreement metric shows how well the sign-based RDM captures the full RDM's structure. Looking at the Qwen3 scale series, the pattern isn't monotonic across model sizes—0.6B and 14B are higher than 4B, which is interesting.

The broader picture across families is messier than it first appears. When I sort all models by size, I see pythia-160m as an outlier at 0.329, then the rest cluster between 0.61 and 0.79. Within Qwen specifically, the progression is non-monotone. So the "0.33 to 0.79" trend I was seeing is really driven by mixing families and the pythia-160m baseline being underdeveloped. The real test is whether Qwen3-32B continues climbing from 14B's 0.793, which would cleanly show whether this metric asymptotes toward 1.0 at larger scales within a single family.

This connects directly to the register gate question—whether the sign and topology components of the operation structure keep dominating as models scale up.

Now I need to clarify what I mean by "register" here. I'm talking about the geometric decomposition of the hidden state into its sign and magnitude components, and how much of the separation in the combinator structure the sign carries. This isn't about semantic routing versus value, spectral properties, or causal mechanisms—it's purely about the topology-share measurement.

The key control concern is whether a rising sep_frac_sign at 32B reflects genuine topological sharpening or could be an artifact. Since this is a positive prediction rather than a falsification, the existing measurement harness already has what I need. The cleanest control is a same-family scale series to remove family confounds. But I should also check whether sep_frac_sign for sign(h) is genuinely elevated compared to what a shuffled or magnitude-matched null would produce. The current harness measures sep_sign and sep_mag directly from real data, but there's no null baseline for the sign-fraction itself. A false version of the claim would be that sign(h) always carries most separation in high-dimensional vectors regardless of training. Testing that would require comparing against a random vector null—which should have near-zero separation on labels anyway.

The real control is the within-family Qwen3 series (0.6B through 32B), where scale is the only varying factor. The prediction is that sep_frac_sign and agree_sign_full at 32B exceed the 14B value of 0.793, trending toward 1.0. But the existing data already shows non-monotonicity (0.742 → 0.667 → 0.793), which weakens a simple monotone-climb narrative. The 32B point becomes genuinely discriminating: if it's above 0.793 with a clean trend, it supports asymptotic behavior toward 1.0; if it plateaus around 0.79 or lower, the topology share stabilizes in the 0.7–0.8 range rather than continuing to climb. Since the forward passes are deterministic given the model, there's no seed variance in the measurement itself beyond the permutation-null seed, so the sep_frac is fully determined by the model.

The right approach is to bootstrap over probes to get a confidence interval on sep_frac_sign, which tells us whether the 14B→32B difference is larger than measurement noise. I can recompute these CIs offline from the saved RDM artifacts (which already contain per-probe data) for all existing models without re-running them. ```
#!/usr/bin/env python3
# register: spectral/semantic
"""Cross-model verdict: the common axis + the topology fraction.

Consumes results/manifold-axis-topology/<model>.{json,npz}.

PART A — IS THERE ONE UNIVERSAL AXIS, AND WHAT DOES IT ENCODE?
  - Build the CONSENSUS prob RDM (mean across models; same probe order) and its
    MDS axis-1 = the candidate universal axis.
  - For each model, take whichever of its 3 MDS axes best matches consensus
    axis-1 (sign-aligned |corr|). High & consistent => one universal axis.
  - Characterize consensus axis-1: combinator eta^2, compositional-depth corr
    (W<I<K<C<B<WHNF<Y<D), next-token entropy corr, prompt-length corr.

PART B — HOW MUCH OF THE MANIFOLD IS TOPOLOGY (sign/routing) vs VALUE (magnitude)?
  - Aggregate per-model: separation fraction carried by sign(h) alone, and
    RDM-reconstruction agreement of sign-only / magnitude-only vs full hidden;
    semantic support(top-64 routing) vs full value RDM. Report scale trend.

Usage: uv run python scripts/experiments/manifold_axis_topology_summary.py
License: MIT
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import json
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np

_PR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent.parent
RESULTS_DIR = _PR / "results" / "manifold-axis-topology"
DEPTH = {"W": 0.90, "I": 1.00, "K": 1.02, "C": 1.05, "B": 1.05,
         "WHNF": 1.09, "Y": 1.14, "D": 1.19}

# rough param size for scale ordering
SIZE = {"pythia-160m": 0.16, "pythia-410m": 0.41, "Qwen3-0.6B": 0.6,
        "SmolLM3-3B": 3, "Qwen3-4B": 4, "Mistral-7B": 7,
        "OLMo-2-1124-13B": 13, "Qwen3-14B": 14}


def family(m):
    m = m.lower()
    for k in ("pythia", "qwen", "mistral", "smollm", "olmo"):
        if k in m:
            return k
    return m


def size_of(m):
    for k, v in SIZE.items():
        if k.lower() in m.lower():
            return v
    return 0


def mds_coords(D, k=3):
    n = D.shape[0]
    J = np.eye(n) - np.ones((n, n)) / n
    B = -0.5 * J @ (D ** 2) @ J
    B = (B + B.T) / 2
    w, V = np.linalg.eigh(B)
    idx = np.argsort(w)[::-1][:k]
    return V[:, idx] * np.sqrt(np.clip(w[idx], 0, None))


def best_axis_match(coords, ref):
    """max |corr| of any of coords' columns with ref (sign-aligned)."""
    best = 0.0
    for j in range(coords.shape[1]):
        c = coords[:, j]
        if np.std(c) < 1e-12:
            continue
        r = np.corrcoef(c, ref)[0, 1]
        if abs(r) > abs(best):
            best = r
    return best


def safe_corr(a, b, m=None):
    if m is not None:
        a, b = a[m], b[m]
    if np.std(a) < 1e-12 or np.std(b) < 1e-12:
        return float("nan")
    return float(np.corrcoef(a, b)[0, 1])


def main():
    models = {}
    for jf in sorted(RESULTS_DIR.glob("*.json")):
        if jf.stem == "summary":
            continue
        meta = json.loads(jf.read_text())
        npz = RESULTS_DIR / f"{jf.stem}.npz"
        if not npz.exists():
            continue
        z = np.load(npz, allow_pickle=True)
        models[meta["model"]] = {"meta": meta, "z": z}
    if len(models) < 2:
        print(f"need >=2 models; found {len(models)}")
        return
    names = list(models.keys())
    print(f"loaded {len(names)} models: {', '.join(family(n) for n in names)}")

    labels = list(models[names[0]]["z"]["labels"])
    lab = np.array(labels)
    depth = np.array([DEPTH.get(x, np.nan) for x in labels])
    dmask = ~np.isnan(depth)

    # ---- PART A: consensus axis ----
    consensus = np.mean([models[n]["z"]["rdm_prob_full"].astype(np.float64)
                         for n in names], axis=0)
    ccoords = mds_coords(consensus, 3)
    cax1 = ccoords[:, 0]
    # entropy averaged across models (z-scored per model first)
    ents = []
    for n in names:
        e = models[n]["z"]["entropy"].astype(np.float64)
        ents.append((e - e.mean()) / (e.std() + 1e-30))
    ent_mean = np.mean(ents, axis=0)
    plen = models[names[0]]["z"]["prompt_len"].astype(np.float64)

    print("\n===== PART A: THE COMMON AXIS =====")
    print("consensus axis-1 encodes:")
    print(f"  eta^2(combinator identity) = {_eta(cax1, lab):.3f}")
    print(f"  corr(compositional depth)  = {safe_corr(cax1, depth, dmask):+.3f}")
    print(f"  corr(next-token entropy)   = {safe_corr(cax1, ent_mean):+.3f}")
    print(f"  corr(prompt length)        = {safe_corr(cax1, plen):+.3f}")
    print("per-model best-axis match to consensus axis-1 (one universal axis?):")
    matches = []
    for n in names:
        m = best_axis_match(models[n]["z"]["axis_coords"].astype(np.float64), cax1)
        matches.append(abs(m))
        print(f"  {family(n):8s} {size_of(n):>5}B  |r|={abs(m):.3f}")
    print(f"  --- mean |match| = {np.mean(matches):.3f} "
          f"(high+consistent => the axis is universal)")

    # ---- PART B: topology fraction ----
    print("\n===== PART B: TOPOLOGY (sign/routing) vs VALUE (magnitude) =====")
    print(f"{'model':24s} {'B':>5s} {'sgnFrac':>7s} {'agrSgn':>7s} {'agrMag':>7s} "
          f"{'supFrac':>7s} {'sepFull':>8s} {'sepSign':>8s}")
    rows = []
    for n in sorted(names, key=size_of):
        t = models[n]["meta"]["topology"]
        supfrac = t["prob_sep_support"] / (t["prob_sep_support"] + t["prob_sep_full"] + 1e-30)
        print(f"{n[:24]:24s} {size_of(n):>5} {t['sep_frac_sign']:7.2f} "
              f"{t['agree_sign_full']:7.3f} {t['agree_mag_full']:7.3f} "
              f"{supfrac:7.2f} {t['sep_full']:8.4f} {t['sep_sign']:8.4f}")
        rows.append({"model": n, "size": size_of(n), **t, "support_frac": supfrac})

    sign_fracs = [r["sep_frac_sign"] for r in rows]
    agr_sign = [r["agree_sign_full"] for r in rows]
    print(f"\n  mean separation fraction in SIGN (topology) = {np.mean(sign_fracs):.2f}")
    print(f"  mean RDM agreement sign-only vs full         = {np.mean(agr_sign):.3f}")
    print("  => fraction of the combinator structure that is purely topological")

    out = {
        "n_models": len(names), "families": sorted({family(n) for n in names}),
        "axis": {
            "eta2_combinator": _eta(cax1, lab),
            "corr_depth": safe_corr(cax1, depth, dmask),
            "corr_entropy": safe_corr(cax1, ent_mean),
            "corr_promptlen": safe_corr(cax1, plen),
            "mean_universal_match": float(np.mean(matches)),
            "per_model_match": {family(n): float(abs(best_axis_match(
                models[n]["z"]["axis_coords"].astype(np.float64), cax1)))
                for n in names},
        },
        "topology": rows,
        "topology_summary": {
            "mean_sign_separation_fraction": float(np.mean(sign_fracs)),
            "mean_agree_sign_full": float(np.mean(agr_sign)),
        },
    }
    (RESULTS_DIR / "summary.json").write_text(json.dumps(out, indent=2))
    print(f"\nwrote {RESULTS_DIR / 'summary.json'}")


def _eta(coord, lab):
    grand = coord.mean()
    ss_tot = ((coord - grand) ** 2).sum() + 1e-30
    ss_b = sum(len(coord[lab == u]) * (coord[lab == u].mean() - grand) ** 2
               for u in set(lab))
    return float(ss_b / ss_tot)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# register: spectral/semantic — common axis + topology decomposition sweep
set -uo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")/../.." || exit 1
LOG="results/manifold-axis-topology/run.log"
mkdir -p results/manifold-axis-topology
PAIRS=(
  "EleutherAI/pythia-160m:float32"
  "EleutherAI/pythia-410m:float32"
  "Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B:bfloat16"
  "HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM3-3B:bfloat16"
  "Qwen/Qwen3-4B:bfloat16"
  "mistralai/Mistral-7B-v0.3:bfloat16"
  "allenai/OLMo-2-1124-13B:bfloat16"
  "Qwen/Qwen3-14B:bfloat16"
)
echo "=== axis+topology sweep start $(date -u +%FT%TZ) ===" | tee -a "$LOG"
for pair in "${PAIRS[@]}"; do
  model="${pair%%:*}"; dtype="${pair##*:}"
  echo "=== $model ($dtype) $(date -u +%FT%TZ) ===" | tee -a "$LOG"
  uv run python scripts/experiments/manifold_axis_topology.py \
      --model "$model" --device mps --dtype "$dtype" --n-perm 2000 2>&1 | tee -a "$LOG"
done
echo "=== axis+topology sweep done $(date -u +%FT%TZ) ===" | tee -a "$LOG"
```

To avoid biasing the statistics, I'll use subsampling instead—drawing 80% of probes without replacement and recomputing the metrics across ~1000 resamples. This approach cleanly sidesteps the duplicate-pair artifact and gives a valid variance estimate. BUT within the clean Qwen3 family it is\nalready NON-MONOTONE: 0.6B=0.742 -> 4B=0.667 -> 14B=0.793. A single 32B bump\nwould be over-read without a measurement-noise control.\n\nTHIS SCRIPT adds the missing control: a SUBSAMPLE confidence interval on each\nquantity, computed offline from the saved RDMs (rdm_hidden_sign / _mag / _full\n+ labels in each <model>.npz) -- so it covers ALL existing models with no\nmodel re-run. We use m-out-of-n subsampling WITHOUT replacement (frac=0.8),\nNOT a with-replacement bootstrap: resampling probes with replacement injects\nduplicate probes -> zero-distance same-label pairs -> deflates the within-class\nmean -> spuriously inflates the separation gap. Subsampling distinct probes\nkeeps every pair a genuine probe-pair.\n\nVerdict logic:\n  - per-model CI on sep_frac_sign and agree_sign_full\n  - within-Qwen3 series (0.6B,4B,8B,14B,32B): linear slope vs log10(size) +\n    Spearman, and whether the 32B CI lies ABOVE the 14B CI (climb), OVERLAPS\n    it (plateau), or below (reversal).\n\nUsage: uv run python scripts/experiments/manifold_topology_ci.py\nLicense: MIT\n\"\"\"\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport json\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nimport numpy as np\n\n_PR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent.parent\nRESULTS_DIR = _PR / \"results\" / \"manifold-axis-topology\"\n\nSIZE = {\"pythia-160m\": 0.16, \"pythia-410m\": 0.41, \"Qwen3-0.6B\": 0.6,\n        \"SmolLM3-3B\": 3, \"Qwen3-4B\": 4, \"Qwen3-8B\": 8, \"Mistral-7B\": 7,\n        \"OLMo-2-1124-13B\": 13, \"Qwen3-14B\": 14, \"Qwen3-32B\": 32}\n\nB = 2000\nFRAC = 0.8\nSEED = 0\n\n\ndef family(m: str) -> str:\n    m = m.lower()\n    for k in (\"pythia\", \"qwen\", \"mistral\", \"smollm\", \"olmo\"):\n        if k in m:\n            return k\n    return m\n\n\ndef size_of(m: str) -> float:\n    for k, v in SIZE.items():\n        if k.lower() in m.lower():\n            return v\n    return 0.0\n\n\ndef upper(D):\n    iu = np.triu_indices_from(D, k=1)\n    return D[iu]\n\n\ndef gap(D, lab):\n    \"\"\"mean(between-class dist) - mean(within-class dist) on off-diagonal pairs.\"\"\"\n    iu = np.triu_indices_from(D, k=1)\n    dv = D[iu]\n    same = lab[iu[0]] == lab[iu[1]]\n    return dv[~same].mean() - dv[same].mean()\n\n\ndef subsample_ci(rdm_sign, rdm_mag, rdm_full, labels, b=B, frac=FRAC, seed=SEED):\n    \"\"\"Subsample distinct probes (m=frac*n, no replacement); recompute\n    sep_frac_sign and agree_sign_full each draw. Returns dict of point + CI.\"\"\"\n    n = len(labels)\n    m = max(8, int(round(frac * n)))\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    fr = np.empty(b)\n    ag = np.empty(b)\n    for t in range(b):\n        idx = rng.choice(n, size=m, replace=False)\n        idx.sort()\n        lab = labels[idx]\n        ss = rdm_sign[np.ix_(idx, idx)]\n        mm = rdm_mag[np.ix_(idx, idx)]\n estimate on the full set\n    gs0 = gap(rdm_sign, labels)\n    gm0 = gap(rdm_mag, labels)\n    frac0 = gs0 / (gs0 + gm0 + 1e-30)\n    agree0 = float(np.corrcoef(upper(rdm_sign), upper(rdm_full))[0, 1])\n    return {\n        \"sep_frac_sign\": float(frac0),\n        \"sep_frac_sign_ci\": [float(np.percentile(fr, 2.5)),\n                             float(np.percentile(fr, 97.5))],\n 97.5))],\n        \"agree_sign_full_sd\": float(ag.std()),\n    }\n\n\ndef main():\n    models = {}\n    for jf in sorted(RESULTS_DIR.glob(\"*.json\")):\n        if jf.stem == \"summary\":\n            continue\n        npz = RESULTS_DIR / f\"{jf.stem}.npz\"\n        if not npz.exists():\n            continue\n        meta = json.loads(jf.read_text())\n        z = np.load(npz, allow_pickle=True)\n        models[meta[\"model\"]] = {\n            \"labels\": np.array([str(x) for x in z[\"labels\"]]),\n            \"rdm_sign\": z[\"rdm_hidden_sign\"].astype(np.float64),\n            \"rdm_mag\": z[\"rdm_hidden_mag\"].astype(np.float64),\n            \"rdm_full\": z[\"rdm_hidden_full\"].astype(np.float64),\n        }\n    if not models:\n        print(\"no models found\")\n        return\n\n    rows = []\n    print(f\"subsample CIs (m={FRAC:.0%} of probes, B={B}, no replacement)\\n\")\n    print(f\"{'model':26s} {'B':>5s} {'sgnFrac':>7s} {'ci95':>15s} \"\n          f\"{'agrSgn':>7s} {'ci95':>15s}\")\n    for name in sorted(models, key=size_of):\n        d = models[name]\n        ci = subsample_ci(d[\"rdm_sign\"], d[\"rdm_mag\"], d[\"rdm_full\"], d[\"labels\"])\n        ci[\"model\"] = name\n        ci[\"size\"] = size_of(name)\n        ci[\"family\"] = family(name)\n        rows.append(ci)\n        fc, fci = ci[\"sep_frac_sign\"], ci[\"sep_frac_sign_ci\"]\n        ac, aci = ci[\"agree_sign_full\"], ci[\"agree_sign_full_ci\"]\n        print(f\"{name[:26]:26s} {size_of(name):>5} {fc:7.3f} \"\n              f\"[{fci[0]:.3f},{fci[1]:.3f}] {ac:7.3f} [{aci[0]:.3f},{aci[1]:.3f}]\")\n\n    # ---- within-Qwen3 scale trend ----\n    qwen = sorted([r for r in rows if r[\"family\"] == \"qwen\"], key=lambda r: r[\"size\"])\n    trend = {}\n    if len(qwen) >= 3:\n        x = np.log10(np.array([r[\"size\"] for r in qwen]))\n        for key in (\"sep_frac_sign\", \"agree_sign_full\"):\n            y = np.array([r[key] for r in qwen])\n            slope = np.polyfit(x, y, 1)[0]\n            # Spearman via rank correlation\n            xr = np.argsort(np.argsort(x))\n            yr = np.argsort(np.argsort(y))\n            rho = float(np.corrcoef(xr, yr)[0, 1])\n            trend[key] = {\"slope_per_decade\": float(slope), \"spearman\": rho,\n                          \"series\": [(r[\"size\"], r[key]) for r in qwen]}\n        # 32B vs 14B CI overlap on sep_frac_sign\n        big = {r[\"size\"]: r for r in qwen}\n        if 32 in big and 14 in big:\n            lo32, hi32 = big[32][\"sep_frac_sign_ci\"]\n            lo14, hi14 = big[14][\"sep_frac_sign_ci\"]\n            if lo32 > hi14:\n                verdict = \"CLIMB (32B CI above 14B CI)\"\n            elif hi32 < lo14:\n                verdict = \"REVERSAL (32B CI below 14B CI)\"\n            else:\n                verdict = \"PLATEAU/NOISE (32B CI overlaps 14B CI)\"\n            trend[\"verdict_32_vs_14\"] = verdict\n\n        print(\"\\n===== within-Qwen3 scale trend (clean family series) =====\")\n        for key in (\"sep_frac_sign\", \"agree_sign_full\"):\n            t = trend[key]\n            ser = \" -> \".join(f\"{s:g}B:{v:.3f}\" for s, v in t[\"series\"])\n            print(f\"  {key:16s}: {ser}\")\n            print(f\"  {'':16s}  slope/decade={t['slope_per_decade']:+.4f} \"\n                  f\"spearman={t['spearman']:+.2f}\")\n        if \"verdict_32_vs_14\" in trend:\n            print(f\"\\n  VERDICT (sep_frac_sign 32B vs 14B): {trend['verdict_32_vs_14']}\")\n    else:\n        print(\"\\n(need >=3 Qwen3 points for a trend; have \"\n              f\"{len(qwen)})\")\n\n    out = {\"params\": {\"B\": B, \"frac\": FRAC, \"seed\": SEED}, \"rows\": rows,\n           \"qwen_trend\": trend}\n    (RESULTS_DIR / \"ci.json\").write_text(json.dumps(out, indent=2))\n    print(f\"\\nwrote {RESULTS_DIR / 'ci.json'}\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"
}

### Thinking

 Looking at the sep_frac_sign metric across Qwen3 models, I'm seeing a pattern that contradicts the expected scaling trend—the values fluctuate without climbing toward 1.0, and there's actually a reversal where the larger 32B model performs worse than the 14B model based on their confidence intervals. The downward slope across model sizes is minimal but consistent, suggesting this metric doesn't improve with scale the way I'd anticipated.

In contrast, agree_sign_full shows a modest positive trend with scale (spearman correlation of 0.90), though the climb is gradual and far from reaching 1.0. The 32B model does outperform 14B on this metric, but only slightly. These two metrics are telling different stories about how sign-based representations scale.

The original claim that sep_frac_sign sharpens monotonically with scale—jumping from 0.33 to 0.79 at 14B—doesn't hold up under scrutiny. The within-family test reveals a noisy plateau around 0.65-0.86 with no clear upward trajectory, which undermines the hypothesis that topology becomes purely dominant at larger scales. What does remain solid is that the topological component is genuinely substantial and real across families, accounting for a meaningful portion of the discrimination signal.

Looking at the 32B results more closely, the magnitude-RDM agreement drops sharply to 0.456 compared to the typical 0.81-0.99 range, suggesting that at very large Qwen scales, magnitude stops being the dominant force in raw geometry. The sign carries more absolute separation at 32B, though the fractional contribution still sits around 0.65 because magnitude contributes substantially too. The original "0.33→0.79" trend was largely an artifact of including the undertrained pythia-160m model; once you exclude that outlier, the rest of the models cluster in the 0.61-0.86 range with no meaningful progression. The clean family series confirms this is just noise around a plateau, not a systematic climb. The disagreement between the two metrics is revealing—sep_frac_sign measures discriminative power while agree_sign_full measures reconstruction fidelity, and they're telling different stories about scale. The lead's own framing was built on sep_frac_sign (the 0.79 at 14B), so the data directly contradicts the "asymptotes to 1.0" claim. Now I need to verify the 32B separation p-value to confirm the run is valid before moving to documentation.

The agree_sign_full metric drifts mildly upward but doesn't rescue the original claim. This is the thirteenth instance of a pattern where the substrate is genuine and crisp, but the optimistic interpretation about scale-driven improvement doesn't hold up.

The core premise stands: operation structure lives in the sign and routing register at around 0.7 and remains stable across scales, which is good for ternary systems, but the idea that ternary becomes purely topological with scale isn't supported. ## Verdict

**Within the clean Qwen3 family (0.6B → 4B → 8B → 14B → 32B):**

| metric | 0.6B | 4B | 8B | 14B | 32B | trend | 32B vs 14B |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sep_frac_sign` | .742 | .667 | .858 | .793 | **.645** | ρ=−0.20, −0.014/dec | **REVERSAL** (CI [.591,.707] below [.751,.838]) |
| `agree_sign_full` | .640 | .712 | .689 | .715 | **.737** | ρ=+0.90, +0.052/dec | mild climb |

- **❌ "topology share asymptotes to 1.0 at scale" — REFUTED.** The defining metric (`sep_frac_sign`, the s211 "0.79@14B" number) shows *no* upward trend and 32B drops to 0.645, significantly below 14B. The s211 "0.33→0.79 sharpening" was an artifact of the single **undercooked pythia-160m (0.33)**; every trained model ≥0.41B sits in a noisy **0.61–0.86 band** with no scale dependence.
- **✅ topology share REAL & substantial but PLATEAUS ~0.7** (cross-family mean 0.67). Sign carries ~65–80% of the combinator *discrimination* while magnitude dominates the raw cosine *geometry* (agrMag 0.81–0.99 ≫ agrSgn ~0.69). The two-registers result holds and is scale-robust — it just doesn't become *purely* topological.
- **◑ `agree_sign_full` has a weak positive drift** (0.64→0.74, ρ=+0.90) but small, far from 1.0, and *disagrees* with `sep_frac_sign` — so it does not rescue the asymptote claim.

This is the **meta-pattern's 13th instance**: substrate real (topology share ~0.7, two registers), the crisp "→1.0 at scale" story over-read. For the north-star: the premise "operation structure lives in the sign/routing register" *holds* at ~0.7 and is **scale-stable** (good enough for ternary), but the optimistic "purely topological at scale = ternary gets better with scale" is **not supported**.

### Thinking

 I'm looking back at the universal axis finding from the knowledge base—it's a single dimension that holds across all language families with a correlation of 0.95, and it's not about the lambda structure. This axis appears to be a generic gradient related to continuation type and predictability, with the strongest correlates being the proportion of function words, punctuation, and whitespace in the top 64 next tokens, plus next-token entropy.

The current proxies explain about 30% of the variance, leaving roughly 70% unaccounted for. The open lead is to characterize what that remaining 70% represents—it's likely tied to deeper distributional properties of the next-token distribution like frequency mass, KL divergence to the unigram baseline, or how tokens rank in a perplexity sweep. The constraint is that only entropy and top-64 indices were saved in the current session, not the full probability vector, so I'd need to re-save the complete distribution to compute these richer features.

The register here is semantic—we're analyzing properties of the next-token probability distribution and how they correlate with this universal MDS axis, which itself comes from the semantic probability RDM.

Looking at what's feasible with the existing saved data: I can compute function-word fraction and entropy from what's there, but features like KL-to-unigram or the concentration of probability mass in the top-64 require the actual probability values, not just the indices. The consensus axis-1 is already available from the saved RDMs across all models, so the target is fixed. What I need is a fresh forward pass to extract richer feature vectors per probe that I can then regress against that axis. corpus base rate, mean log-frequency of top tokens to see if likely continuations are common function words or rare content, and the fraction of mass on whitespace/punctuation in the top-64. I could also look at prompt-level features like length and whether it ends with punctuation. Actually, the cleanest approach might be to test whether the axis is fundamentally a property of the prompts themselves rather than the models — if prompt-intrinsic features (length, syntax patterns, last-token type) can predict the axis well, that would directly support the claim that this universal structure reflects properties of language. For the immediate task though, I'll focus on computing richer distributional features during forward passes on a subset of models and saving just those features rather than full probability distributions, then regress against the consensus axis.

```
#!/usr/bin/env python3
# register: semantic
"""Name the universal axis of the combinator manifold.

manifold_axis_topology.py found ONE strongly universal axis (consensus MDS
axis-1 of the next-token-probability RDM; per-model best-axis match |r|=0.95
across 5 families, 0.16B->14B). But it is NOT the combinator operations
(eta^2=0.05), NOT compositional depth (r=-0.01), NOT prompt length (r=-0.02);
its best single correlate was next-token entropy (r=-0.29, modest).

HYPOTHESIS (register: semantic): the universal axis is a GENERIC PREDICTABILITY
/ CONTINUATION-TYPE gradient — does the prompt resolve toward a peaked, generic
continuation (function word / punctuation / high-frequency token) or a diffuse /
content-specific one? Test by regressing axis-1 on:
  - entropy            : H(next-token)               (diffuseness)
  - top1_function      : is argmax token a function word / punct / whitespace?
  - topk_function_frac : fraction of top-64 that are function/punct/space tokens
  - prompt_len         : token count                 (confound)
  - combinator (eta^2) : operation identity          (control)
Univariate corr + a multivariate R^2; if {entropy, function-continuation} carry
most of the axis, the axis is NAMED: generic predictability, not lambda structure.

OFFLINE: reads results/manifold-axis-topology/<model>.npz (axis_coords, entropy,
topk indices, prompt_len, labels) + each model's AutoTokenizer (no weights).

Usage: uv run python scripts/experiments/axis_probe.py
License: MIT
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import json
import string
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
from transformers import AutoTokenizer

_PR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent.parent
RESULTS_DIR = _PR / "results" / "manifold-axis-topology"
OUT = RESULTS_DIR / "axis_probe.json"

# English function words (closed-class): the part of a continuation that is
# grammatical glue rather than content.
STOP = {
    "the", "a", "an", "and", "or", "but", "of", "to", "in", "on", "at", "for",
    "with", "by", "from", "as", "is", "are", "was", "were", "be", "been",
    "being", "it", "its", "this", "that", "these", "those", "he", "she", "they",
    "we", "you", "i", "his", "her", "their", "our", "your", "my", "him", "them",
    "us", "not", "no", "nor", "so", "if", "then", "than", "which", "who", "whom",
    "whose", "what", "when", "where", "why", "how", "all", "any", "some", "can",
    "will", "would", "could", "should", "may", "might", "must", "have", "has",
    "had", "do", "does", "did", "s", "t", "re", "ll", "ve", "m", "d", "into",
    "out", "up", "down", "over", "under", "about", "after", "before", "between",
    "there", "here", "one", "two", "more", "most", "such", "only", "also", "very",
}
PUNCT = set(string.punctuation) | {"“", "”", "‘", "’", "—", "–", "…", "·"}


def family(m):
    m = m.lower()
    for k in ("pythia", "qwen", "mistral", "smollm", "olmo"):
        if k in m:
            return k
    return m


def classify_token(s: str) -> str:
    """function | content | punct | space — from the decoded token string."""
    raw = s
    t = s.strip().lower()
    if raw.strip() == "":
        return "space"
    if all((ch in PUNCT or ch.isspace()) for ch in t) and t != "":
        return "punct"
    # strip a leading subword marker space; keep alnum core
    core = "".join(ch for ch in t if ch.isalnum())
    if core == "":
        return "punct"
    if core in STOP:
        return "function"
    return "content"


def mds_axis1(D):
    n = D.shape[0]
    J = np.eye(n) - np.ones((n, n)) / n
    B = -0.5 * J @ (D ** 2) @ J
    B = (B + B.T) / 2
    w, V = np.linalg.eigh(B)
    j = int(np.argmax(w))
    return V[:, j] * np.sqrt(max(w[j], 0.0))


def corr(a, b):
    a = np.asarray(a, float); b = np.asarray(b, float)
    if np.std(a) < 1e-12 or np.std(b) < 1e-12:
        return float("nan")
    return float(np.corrcoef(a, b)[0, 1])


def multi_r2(y, X):
    """OLS R^2 of y ~ [1, X] (X columns z-scored)."""
    y = np.asarray(y, float)
    Xz = []
    for col in X:
        c = np.asarray(col, float)
        c = (c - c.mean()) / (c.std() + 1e-12)
        Xz.append(c)
    A = np.column_stack([np.ones_like(y)] + Xz)
    beta, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)
    pred = A @ beta
    ss_res = ((y - pred) ** 2).sum()
    ss_tot = ((y - y.mean()) ** 2).sum() + 1e-30
    return float(1 - ss_res / ss_tot), beta[1:].tolist()


def eta2(coord, lab):
    lab = np.asarray(lab)
    grand = coord.mean()
    ss_tot = ((coord - grand) ** 2).sum() + 1e-30
    ss_b = sum(len(coord[lab == u]) * (coord[lab == u].mean() - grand) ** 2
               for u in set(lab))
    return float(ss_b / ss_tot)


def features_for_model(model, z, tok):
    labels = list(z["labels"])
    topk = z["topk"]                       # [N x 64] token ids
    ent = z["entropy"].astype(np.float64)
    plen = z["prompt_len"].astype(np.float64)
    n, k = topk.shape
    # decode unique ids once
    uniq = np.unique(topk)
    cls = {int(i): classify_token(tok.decode([int(i)])) for i in uniq}
    top1_function = np.zeros(n)
    topk_function_frac = np.zeros(n)
    for i in range(n):
        cats = [cls[int(t)] for t in topk[i]]
        top1_function[i] = 1.0 if cats[0] in ("function", "punct", "space") else 0.0
        topk_function_frac[i] = np.mean([c in ("function", "punct", "space")
                                         for c in cats])
    return {"labels": labels, "entropy": ent, "prompt_len": plen,
            "top1_function": top1_function,
            "topk_function_frac": topk_function_frac}


def main():
    files = [f for f in sorted(RESULTS_DIR.glob("*.json"))
             if f.stem not in ("summary", "axis_probe")]
    models = {}
    for jf in files:
        meta = json.loads(jf.read_text())
        npz = RESULTS_DIR / f"{jf.stem}.npz"
        if not npz.exists():
            continue
        models[meta["model"]] = np.load(npz, allow_pickle=True)
    if not models:
        print("no per-model npz found")
        return
    names = list(models.keys())
    print(f"loaded {len(names)} models: {', '.join(family(n) for n in names)}")

    # consensus axis-1 from mean prob RDM
    consensus = np.mean([models[n]["rdm_prob_full"].astype(np.float64)
                         for n in names], axis=0)
    cax1 = mds_axis1(consensus)

    # per-model features; build consensus (z-scored, averaged) features
    feats = {n: features_for_model(n, models[n], AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(n))
             for n in names}
    labels = feats[names[0]]["labels"]

    def consensus_feature(key):
        vals = []
        for n in names:
            v = feats[n][key].astype(np.float64)
            vals.append((v - v.mean()) / (v.std() + 1e-12))
        return np.mean(vals, axis=0)

    cf = {k: consensus_feature(k) for k in
          ("entropy", "top1_function", "topk_function_frac", "prompt_len")}

    print("\n===== CONSENSUS AXIS-1 — what explains it? =====")
    rows = {}
    for k, v in cf.items():
        r = corr(cax1, v)
        rows[k] = r
        print(f"  corr(axis1, {k:18s}) = {r:+.3f}")
    print(f"  eta^2(combinator identity)      = {eta2(cax1, labels):.3f}  (control)")
    r2_pred, beta = multi_r2(cax1, [cf["entropy"], cf["top1_function"],
                                    cf["topk_function_frac"]])
    print(f"  multivariate R^2 [entropy + top1_function + topk_function_frac] "
          f"= {r2_pred:.3f}")
    r2_full, _ = multi_r2(cax1, [cf["entropy"], cf["top1_function"],
                                 cf["topk_function_frac"], cf["prompt_len"]])
    print(f"  + prompt_len -> R^2 = {r2_full:.3f}")

    print("\n===== PER-MODEL (each model's own axis-1 vs its own features) =====")
    print(f"{'model':24s} {'ent':>6s} {'t1fn':>6s} {'kfn':>6s} {'R2':>6s} {'eta2':>6s}")
    per = {}
    for n in names:
        ax = models[n]["axis_coords"][:, 0].astype(np.float64)
        f = feats[n]
        r2, _ = multi_r2(ax, [f["entropy"], f["top1_function"],
                              f["topk_function_frac"]])
        per[n] = {"entropy": corr(ax, f["entropy"]),
                  "top1_function": corr(ax, f["top1_function"]),
                  "topk_function_frac": corr(ax, f["topk_function_frac"]),
                  "r2": r2, "eta2_combinator": eta2(ax, f["labels"])}
        print(f"{n[:24]:24s} {per[n]['entropy']:+6.2f} {per[n]['top1_function']:+6.2f} "
              f"{per[n]['topk_function_frac']:+6.2f} {r2:6.2f} "
              f"{per[n]['eta2_combinator']:6.2f}")

    out = {"n_models": len(names), "families": sorted({family(n) for n in names}),
           "consensus": {"corr": rows, "eta2_combinator": eta2(cax1, labels),
                         "r2_pred": r2_pred, "r2_with_len": r2_full,
                         "beta_pred": beta},
           "per_model": {family(n): per[n] for n in names}}
    OUT.write_text(json.dumps(out, indent=2))
    print(f"\nwrote {OUT}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

### Thinking

 I'm understanding the s211 axis_probe structure now—the consensus axis-1 comes from averaging the probability RDM across models using MDS, and it's available offline from saved data. The model uses four features (entropy, top1_function, topk_function_frac, prompt_len) achieving a multivariate R² of 0.296, but there's a limitation: the saved npz file only stores the top-64 indices, not their actual probability values, so I can't derive mass-based features from the saved data alone.

To capture richer distributional properties of the next-token predictions, I'd need to run fresh forward passes and save the full distribution. The candidates I'm considering include peak probability (max prob), cumulative mass at different k values to understand concentration shape, KL divergence from unigram baseline to measure how much the prompt constrains the prediction, probability-weighted mass on function/punctuation tokens versus content tokens, mean log frequency of predicted tokens, and alternative entropy measures like Rényi entropy that capture peakedness differently. I'm also thinking about prompt-intrinsic features like length and whether it ends with an operator or punctuation mark.

The cleanest test would be whether model-free prompt features alone can predict the universal axis — that would be a strong, independent signal. But I'm also noticing something important: entropy showed inconsistent correlations with the consensus axis across models (pythia −0.05, smollm +0.41, qwen +0.31, olmo +0.39, mistral +0.03), which suggests either the relationship isn't stable or there's a sign-alignment issue when averaging per-model features against a consensus axis.

Looking at the R² values per model (pythia 0.28, smollm 0.20, qwen 0.12, mistral 0.44, olmo 0.23), the multivariate R² on consensus is only 0.30, leaving most variance unexplained. My next move is to compute richer distributional features — mass-based metrics, KL divergence to unigram, top-1 probability, collision entropy — and regress the consensus axis against this expanded feature set to see if I can explain more. The challenge is getting unigram frequencies: I could use the marginal distribution from the 535 probes themselves as a proxy, or pull from a corpus.

I'm settling on features computable directly from the probability distribution and tokenizer without external data: top-1 probability for confidence, concentration metrics like top-5/10/64 mass, collision entropy for peakedness, function-word and content-word mass weighted by probability, effective support size, and prompt-intrinsic features like length and punctuation. For KL divergence, I'll use each model's average next-token distribution across the 535 probes as the reference, which naturally captures how distinctive each prediction is relative to the model's typical behavior.

The challenge is computing KL-to-mean efficiently—I need the mean distribution first, then each individual probability to calculate divergence. I can either save all 535 probability vectors in RAM (about 321MB per model, which is manageable), compute the mean and all features offline, then discard the raw probabilities and keep only the features. This avoids storing huge files and lets me process models sequentially. I'm thinking through which models to include—aiming for a representative subset across families and scales to keep the analysis tractable. The saved data is mostly exhausted at R²=0.30, so I need fresh forward passes to compute the distributional features like top1_prob and KL divergence. I'll split this into two parts: first, extract prompt-intrinsic features offline (length, punctuation, token class patterns) to test if the axis correlates with language properties alone, then run forward passes on the 6 models to get the richer distributional features and measure their incremental R². The forward passes should take 15-20 minutes total, which is manageable.

For the implementation, I'll create a harness that runs forward passes on the 535 probes per model, computes the feature matrix, and saves it alongside the existing saved data. Then a summary script loads the consensus axis from the saved probability distributions, builds consensus features from the same model set, adds the prompt-intrinsic features, and reports incremental R² gains across the feature blocks. I'll keep the model set consistent—using the 6 models I'm running rich features on—so the consensus axis and features are built from the same data.

I'm settling on 8 models to balance coverage and speed: pythia-410m, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-4B, SmolLM3-3B, Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B, OLMo-13B, and Qwen3-14B. This mirrors the original consensus set but swaps out the undercooked 160m model for the 8B variant and keeps the 14B for fuller Qwen scale coverage.

Computing features per probe...

For function and content mass, I'll classify only the top-256 tokens per probe since glue tokens cluster there anyway, making the tail negligible. Rather than decode 256 tokens across all 535 probes repeatedly, I'll collect the union of unique top-256 token IDs and classify them once. I'm also holding all 535 full probability vectors in RAM to compute the mean distribution afterward, then calculating KL divergence from each probe's distribution to that mean—this captures how distinctive each continuation is relative to the average. I'll skip KL-to-uniform since it's redundant with entropy. For prompt-level features, I'm tracking character length and word count offline from the probe library.

Now I'm adding a few more token-level features: whether the prompt ends with punctuation or whitespace, if it contains lambda glyphs, and whether the last word is a function name. Before diving into the full regression, though, I should sanity-check whether this approach risks over-engineering. The real question is whether these distributional features can explain the remaining ~70% of variance in the consensus axis. If adding top-1 probability, function mass, and KL-to-mean pushes R² from 0.30 to 0.6+, we've identified a meaningful signal. If it stalls around 0.3–0.4, that tells us the axis is genuinely high-dimensional and not reducible to simple scalar proxies—which is itself a valuable finding. Either way, I'll set up the regression with proper controls: adjusted R², cross-validated R², and a permutation null to guard against overfitting on 535 points with ~10–15 features.

My plan is to build a harness that computes these rich distributional features across all 8 models, then run a summary analysis offline with hierarchical R² reporting and null tests. I'll start by writing the feature extraction code, then launch it in parallel while building the summary pipeline. One thing to watch: for diffuse distributions with high entropy, the top-256 tokens might not capture the full mass, but for the glue tokens and common content words that drive the axis, this should be sufficient.

Writing axis_naming.py...

I now fully understand the s211 method: consensus axis-1 from the mean prob-RDM, regressed on `{entropy, top1_function, topk_function_frac}` → **R²=0.296**. Critically, **the npz saved only top-64 token *indices*, not the probabilities** — so all mass-based features (top-1 prob, prob-weighted glue mass, KL) require fresh forward passes. That matches the open lead's note.

## Step 0 — REGISTER GATE

**Register: `semantic`.** The object is the consensus MDS axis-1 of the next-token **probability** RDM, regressed on properties of the next-token **distribution** (+ model-free prompt-text features). Not geometric, spectral, or causal. Control header: `# register: semantic`. 

**Discriminating control built in:** with ~12 features over 535 points, R² can be inflated by overfitting — so I'll report **k-fold cross-validated R²** and a **permutation null** (shuffle axis-1 vs features), not just in-sample R². A "named axis" only counts if CV-R² and the null gap hold.

Let me plan and build.

### Thinking

 I'm setting up the harness to reuse the token classification logic, then building axis_naming.py. I need a function that generates probes with their prompts and labels, runs a forward pass for each probe to get the full probability vector, and stores everything in a large float32 array. Then I'm computing features by classifying the top-512 token IDs per probe and summing probabilities across categories like function, content, and punctuation.

For KL divergence to the mean, I'm computing the mean probability distribution across all probes, then calculating KL for each probe against that mean using only the support where probabilities are non-zero. I'm handling this efficiently with numpy operations on the full vocabulary.

Now I'm optimizing the vectorized computation: sorting each row of the probability matrix to get entropy, top-1 probability, and cumulative mass for the n90 metric. I'm using argpartition to extract the top-512 indices per probe for classification, and computing collision as negative log of the sum of squared probabilities. Memory-wise, the peak usage should stay under 1GB with the probability matrix, sorted copy, and partition results.

enumerate(prompts):\n        enc = tokenizer(text, return_tensors=\"pt\", truncation=True,\n                        max_length=max_length)\n        enc = {k: v.to(device) for k, v in enc.items()}\n        out = model(**enc)\n        logits = out.logits[0, -1].float()\n        p = torch.softmax(logits, dim=-1).cpu().numpy().astype(np.float32)\n        if probs is None:\n            probs = np.empty((n, p.shape[0]), np.float32)\n        probs[i] = p\n        plen[i] = int(enc[\"input_ids\"].shape[1])\n        del out, logits\n        if (i + 1) % 100 == 0:\n            log(f\"    {i + 1}/{n}\")\n    return probs, plen\n\n\ndef compute_features(probs, plen, tokenizer):\n    \"\"\"probs [N x V] -> features [N x F] (order = FEATURE_NAMES).\"\"\"\n    n, V = probs.shape\n    P = probs.astype(np.float64)\n    Pc = np.clip(P, 1e-30, None)\n\n    entropy = -(P * np.log(Pc)).sum(1)\n    collision = -np.log(np.clip((P ** 2).sum(1), 1e-30, None))\n    top1 = P.max(1)\n\n    log(\"    sorting for mass curve + n90 ...\")\n    S = -np.sort(-P, axis=1)                       # descending\n    csum = np.cumsum(S, axis=1)\n    def kmass(k):\n        return S[:, :k].sum(1)\n    top5, top10, top32 = kmass(5), kmass(10), kmass(32)\n    top64, top256 = kmass(64), kmass(256)\n    n90 = (csum < 0.9).sum(1) + 1                   # tokens to reach 0.9 mass\n\n    log(\"    classifying top-512 token ids ...\")\n    topidx = np.argpartition(-P, CLASSIFY_TOPN, axis=1)[:, :CLASSIFY_TOPN]\n    uniq = np.unique(topidx)\n    cls = {int(t): classify_token(tokenizer.decode([int(t)])) for t in uniq}\n    is_fn = np.array([cls[int(t)] in (\"function\", \"space\") for t in uniq])\n    is_pn = np.array([cls[int(t)] == \"punct\" for t in uniq])\n    is_ct = np.array([cls[int(t)] == \"content\" for t in uniq])\n    id2pos = {int(t): j for j, t in enumerate(uniq)}\n    function_mass = np.zeros(n); content_mass = np.zeros(n)\n    punct_mass = np.zeros(n); captured = np.zeros(n)\n pp[is_pn[pos]].sum()\n        content_mass[i] = pp[is_ct[pos]].sum()\n        captured[i] = pp.sum()\n\n    log(\"    KL to mean (generic-continuation distinctiveness) ...\")\n    mean_p = P.mean(0)\n    log_mean = np.log(np.clip(mean_p, 1e-30, None))\n    kl = (P * (np.log(Pc) - log_mean[None, :])).sum(1)\n\n    feats = np.column_stack([\n        entropy, collision, top1, top5, top10, top32, top64, top256,\n        n90.astype(np.float64), function_mass, content_mass, punct_mass,\n        captured, kl,\n    ])\n    assert feats.shape[1] == len(FEATURE_NAMES)\n    return feats\n\n\ndef main():\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()\n    ap.add_argument(\"--model\", required=True)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--device\", default=\"mps\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--dtype\", default=\"bfloat16\",\n                    choices=[\"float32\", \"float16\", \"bfloat16\"])\n    ap.add_argument(\"--max-length\", type=int, default=64)\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n\n    RESULTS_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    safe = args.model.replace(\"/\", \"_\")\n    t0 = time.time()\n\n    probes = crystal_probes()\n    prompts = [p.prompt for p in probes]\n    labels = [p.combinator for p in probes]\n    log(f\"[{args.model}] {len(prompts)} probes\")\n\n    dtype = {\"float32\": torch.float32, \"float16\": torch.float16,\n             \"bfloat16\": torch.bfloat16}[args.dtype]\n    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(args.model)\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(args.model, torch_dtype=dtype)\n    model.to(args.device).eval()\n    log(\"  forward passes (full dist held in RAM) ...\")\n    probs, plen = collect_probs(model, tok, args.device, prompts, args.max_length)\n    vocab = int(probs.shape[1])\n    del model\n    gc.collect()\n    if args.device == \"mps\":\n        torch.mps.empty_cache()\n\n    log(\"  computing rich distributional features ...\")\n    feats = compute_features(probs, plen, tok)\n    del probs\n    gc.collect()\n\n    out = {\"model\": args.model, \"dtype\": args.dtype, \"n_probes\": len(prompts),\n           \"vocab\": vocab, \"feature_names\": FEATURE_NAMES, \"git_sha\": git_sha(),\n           \"elapsed_s\": round(time.time() - t0, 1),\n           \"feature_means\": {k: float(feats[:, j].mean())\n                             for j, k in enumerate(FEATURE_NAMES)}}\n    np.savez_compressed(RESULTS_DIR / f\"{safe}.features.npz\",\n                        features=feats.astype(np.float32),\n                        feature_names=np.array(FEATURE_NAMES),\n  The sweep is progressing well—pythia-410m finished quickly and SmolLM3-3B is running now. While that's happening, I'm moving on to build the summary script since task 3 is no longer blocked. I need to pull the consensus axis-1 from the saved rdm_prob_full data for the model set I'm currently running.

For the summary script, I need both the original model npz files (which contain rdm_prob_full and axis coordinates) and the features npz files (for the rich features), matched by model name. I'll reconstruct the baseline features that s211 used—entropy, top1_function, and topk_function_frac—by recomputing them from the topk indices in the original npz files using the tokenizer, just like axis_probe did. This way I can make a direct comparison against s211's baseline R² of 0.296, even though my model set differs slightly from theirs.

I'm designing a hierarchical feature block structure: starting with the s211 baseline (entropy, top1_function, topk_function_frac), then adding distributional shape features like top1_prob and collision metrics, then glue-mass features (function_mass, content_mass, punct_mass), then distinctiveness (kl_to_mean), and finally prompt-intrinsic features like prompt length and punctuation patterns. For each cumulative block I'll compute in-sample R², 5-fold cross-validation R², and permutation null R² with 1000 shuffles. Since n90 has a heavy right skew (ranging from 1 to 13680), I'll apply a log1p transformation to handle that before z-scoring, while keeping other features as-is.

For the prompt-intrinsic features, I'll extract them directly from the probe library: token-based prompt length (already available), word count from splitting, whether the prompt ends with punctuation or whitespace, presence of lambda symbols, and whether the last word is in the function stop list. I'll handle cross-validation properly by z-scoring within each fold using only training statistics to avoid leakage, then compute pooled out-of-fold R² across all test points. For the permutation null, I'll shuffle y and recompute the full model's CV-R² across 1000 iterations to establish a baseline for overfitting.

__future__ import annotations\n\nimport json\nimport string\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nimport numpy as np\nfrom transformers import AutoTokenizer\n\nfrom verbum.probes.library import crystal_probes\n\n_PR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent.parent\nRESULTS_DIR = _PR / \"results\" / \"manifold-axis-topology\"\nOUT = RESULTS_DIR / \"axis_naming.json\"\n\nSTOP = {\n    \"the\", \"a\", \"an\", \"and\", \"or\", \"but\", \"of\", \"to\", \"in\", \"on\", \"at\", \"for\",\n    \"with\", \"by\", \"from\", \"as\", \"is\", \"are\", \"was\", \"were\", \"be\", \"been\",\n    \"being\", \"it\", \"its\", \"this\", \"that\", \"these\", \"those\", \"he\", \"she\", \"they\",\n    \"we\", \"you\", \"i\", \"his\", \"her\", \"their\", \"our\", \"your\", \"my\", \"him\", \"them\",\n    \"us\", \"not\", \"no\", \"nor\", \"so\", \"if\", \"then\", \"than\", \"which\", \"who\", \"whom\",\n    \"whose\", \"what\", \"when\", \"where\", \"why\", \"how\", \"all\", \"any\", \"some\", \"can\",\n    \"will\", \"would\", \"could\", \"should\", \"may\", \"might\", \"must\", \"have\", \"has\",\n    \"had\", \"do\", \"does\", \"did\", \"s\", \"t\", \"re\", \"ll\", \"ve\", \"m\", \"d\", \"into\",\n    \"out\", \"up\", \"down\", \"over\", \"under\", \"about\", \"after\", \"before\", \"between\",\n    \"there\", \"here\", \"one\", \"two\", \"more\", \"most\", \"such\", \"only\", \"also\", \"very\",\n}\nPUNCT = set(string.punctuation) | {\"\\u201c\", \"\\u201d\", \"\\u2018\", \"\\u2019\",\n                                   \"\\u2014\", \"\\u2013\", \"\\u2026\", \"\\u00b7\"}\n\nBLOCKS = [\n    (\"B0_s211_baseline\", [\"entropy\", \"top1_function\", \"topk_function_frac\"]),\n    (\"B1_peakedness\", [\"top1_prob\", \"top10_mass\", \"collision\", \"log_n90\",\n                       \"top256_mass\"]),\n    (\"B2_glue_mass\", [\"function_mass\", \"content_mass\", \"punct_mass\"]),\n    (\"B3_distinctiveness\", [\"kl_to_mean\"]),\n    (\"B4_prompt_only\", [\"n_words\", \"ends_punct\", \"ends_space\", \"has_lambda\",\n                        \"last_word_fn\"]),\n]\n\n\ndef family(m):\n    m = m.lower()\n    for k in (\"pythia\", \"qwen\", \"mistral\", \"smollm\", \"olmo\"):\n        if k in m:\n            return k\n    return m\n\n\ndef classify_token(s):\n    t = s.strip().lower()\n    if s.strip() == \"\":\n        return \"space\"\n    if all((ch in PUNCT or ch.isspace()) for ch in t) and t != \"\":\n        return \"punct\"\n    core = \"\".join(ch for ch in t if ch.isalnum())\n    if core == \"\":\n        return \"punct\"\n    if core in STOP:\n        return \"function\"\n    return \"content\"\n\n\ndef mds_axis1(D):\n    n = D.shape[0]\n    J = np.eye(n) - np.ones((n, n)) / n\n    B = -0.5 * J @ (D ** 2) @ J\n    B = (B + B.T) / 2\n    w, V = np.linalg.eigh(B)\n    j = int(np.argmax(w))\n    return V[:, j] * np.sqrt(max(w[j], 0.0))\n\n\ndef corr(a, b):\n    a = np.asarray(a, float); b = np.asarray(b, float)\n    if np.std(a) < 1e-12 or np.std(b) < 1e-12:\n        return float(\"nan\")\n    return float(np.corrcoef(a, b)[0, 1])\n\n\ndef _fit_r2(ytr, Xtr, yte, Xte):\n    \"\"\"OLS on train (standardized by train stats), R^2 on test.\"\"\"\n    mu = Xtr.mean(0); sd = Xtr.std(0) + 1e-12\n    Atr = np.column_stack([np.ones(len(ytr)), (Xtr - mu) / sd])\n    beta, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(Atr, ytr, rcond=None)\n    Ate = np.column_stack([np.ones(len(yte)), (Xte - mu) / sd])\n    pred = Ate @ beta\n    ss_res = ((yte - pred) ** 2).sum()\n    ss_tot = ((yte - yte.mean()) ** 2).sum() + 1e-30\n    return 1 - ss_res / ss_tot\n\n\ndef in_sample_r2(y, X):\n    return _fit_r2(y, X, y, X)\n\n\ndef cv_r2(y, X, k=5, seed=0):\n    \"\"\"Out-of-fold pooled R^2.\"\"\"\n    n = len(y)\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)\n    order = rng.permutation(n)\n    folds = np.array_split(order, k)\n    pred = np.empty(n)\n    for f in folds:\n        mask = np.ones(n, bool); mask[f] = False\n        mu = X[mask].mean(0); sd = X[mask].std(0) + 1e-12\n        Atr = np.column_stack([np.ones(mask.sum()), (X[mask] - mu) / sd])\n        beta, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(Atr, y[mask], rcond=None)\n        Ate = np.column_stack([np.ones(len(f)), (X[f] - mu) / sd])\n        pred[f] = Ate @ beta\n    ss_res = ((y - pred) ** 2).sum()\n    ss_tot = ((y - y.mean()) ** 2).sum() + 1e-30\n    return 1 - ss_res / ss_tot\n\n\ndef main():\n    # ---- load models that have BOTH npz and features.npz ----\n    models = {}\n    for jf in sorted(RESULTS_DIR.glob(\"*.features.json\")):\n        name = json.loads(jf.read_text())[\"model\"]\n        safe = name.replace(\"/\", \"_\")\n        base = RESULTS_DIR / f\"{safe}.npz\"\n        feat = RESULTS_DIR / f\"{safe}.features.npz\"\n        if not (base.exists() and feat.exists()):\n            continue\n        z = np.load(base, allow_pickle=True)\n        zf = np.load(feat, allow_pickle=True)\n        models[name] = {\"z\": z, \"zf\": zf}\n    if len(models) < 2:\n        print(f\"need >=2 models with features; found {len(models)}\")\n        return\n    names = list(models.keys())\n    print(f\"loaded {len(names)} models: {', '.join(family(n) for n in names)}\")\n\n    labels = [str(x) for x in models[names[0]][\"zf\"][\"labels\"]]\n    fnames = [str(x) for x in models[names[0]][\"zf\"][\"feature_names\"]]\n    fidx = {f: j for j, f in enumerate(fnames)}\n    N = len(labels)\n\n    # consensus axis-1\n    consensus = np.mean([models[n][\"z\"][\"rdm_prob_full\"].astype(np.float64)\n                         for n in names], axis=0)\n    cax1 = mds_axis1(consensus)\n\n    # ---- prompt-intrinsic (model-free) features ----\n    probes = crystal_probes()\n    prompts = [p.prompt for p in probes]\n    assert len(prompts) == N\n    ref_tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(names[0])  # only for parity; not used\n    n_words = np.array([len(p.split()) for p in prompts], float)\n    def last_char(p):\n        s = p.rstrip()\n        return s[-1] if s else \"\"\n    ends_punct = np.array([1.0 if last_char(p) in PUNCT else 0.0 for p in prompts])\n    ends_space = np.array([1.0 if (p and p[-1].isspace()) else 0.0 for p in prompts])\n    has_lambda = np.array([1.0 if (\"\\u03bb\" in p or \"\\\\\" in p) else 0.0\n                           for p in prompts])\n    def last_word_fn(p):\n        ws = p.split()\n        if not ws:\n            return 0.0\n        core = \"\".join(ch for ch in ws[-1].lower() if ch.isalnum())\n        return 1.0 if core in STOP else 0.0\n    last_word_fn_v = np.array([last_word_fn(p) for p in prompts])\n    prompt_feats = {\"n_words\": n_words, \"ends_punct\": ends_punct,\n                    \"ends_space\": ends_space, \"has_lambda\": has_lambda,\n                    \"last_word_fn\": last_word_fn_v}\n\n    # ---- per-model feature dict, then consensus (z-scored mean) ----\n    def zscore(v):\n        v = np.asarray(v, float)\n        return (v - v.mean()) / (v.std() + 1e-12)\n\n    # per-model rich + baseline-function features\n    per_model_feat = {n: {} for n in names}\n    for n in names:\n        zf = models[n][\"zf\"]; F = zf[\"features\"].astype(np.float64)\n        for f in fnames:\n            per_model_feat[n][f] = F[:, fidx[f]]\n        per_model_feat[n][\"log_n90\"] = np.log1p(per_model_feat[n][\"n90\"])\n        # s211 baseline function features from original-npz top-64 indices\n        topk = models[n][\"z\"][\"topk\"]\n        tk = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(n)\n        uniq = np.unique(topk)\n        cls = {int(i): classify_token(tk.decode([int(i)])) for i in uniq}\n        t1 = np.zeros(N); kf = np.zeros(N)\n        for i in range(N):\n            cats = [cls[int(t)] for t in topk[i]]\n            t1[i] = 1.0 if cats[0] in (\"function\", \"punct\", \"space\") else 0.0\n            kf[i] = np.mean([c in (\"function\", \"punct\", \"space\") for c in cats])\n        per_model_feat[n][\"top1_function\"] = t1\n        per_model_feat[n][\"topk_function_frac\"] = kf\n\n    model_dependent = set(fnames) | {\"log_n90\", \"top1_function\",\n                                     \"topk_function_frac\"}\n\n    def consensus_feature(key):\n        if key in prompt_feats:                  # model-free\n            return zscore(prompt_feats[key])\n        return np.mean([zscore(per_model_feat[n][key]) for n in names], axis=0)\n\n    needed = sorted({f for _, fs in BLOCKS for f in fs})\n    cf = {k: consensus_feature(k) for k in needed}\n\n    # ---- univariate corr (diagnostic) ----\n    print(\"\\n===== univariate corr(axis1, feature) =====\")\n    uni = {}\n    for k in needed:\n        r = corr(cax1, cf[k]); uni[k] = r\n        print(f\"  {k:20s} {r:+.3f}\")\n\n    # ---- hierarchical blocks ----\n    print(\"\\n===== cumulative R^2 (in-sample / 5-fold CV) =====\")\n    used = []\n    blocks_out = []\n    for bname, fs in BLOCKS:\n        used = used + fs\n        X = np.column_stack([cf[k] for k in used])\n        r2 = in_sample_r2(cax1, X)\n        cvr2 = cv_r2(cax1, X)\n        blocks_out.append({\"block\": bname, \"features_added\": fs,\n                           \"n_features\": len(used),\n                           \"r2_insample\": r2, \"r2_cv\": cvr2})\n        print(f\"  {bname:20s} (+{len(fs)}, tot {len(used):2d})  \"\n              f\"R2={r2:.3f}  CV-R2={cvr2:.3f}\")\n\n    # ---- permutation null on the FULL model CV-R^2 ----\n    Xfull = np.column_stack([cf[k] for k in used])\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)\n    B = 200\n    null = np.array([cv_r2(rng.permutation(cax1), Xfull) for _ in range(B)])\n    full_cv = blocks_out[-1][\"r2_cv\"]\n    p = float((np.sum(null >= full_cv) + 1) / (B + 1))\n    print(f\"\\n  FULL CV-R2 = {full_cv:.3f}  vs permutation null \"\n          f\"{null.mean():+.3f} ± {null.std():.3f} (95th {np.percentile(null,95):+.3f})  p={p:.3f}\")\n\n    out = {\"n_models\": len(names), \"families\": sorted({family(n) for n in names}),\n           \"models\": names, \"n_probes\": N,\n           \"univariate_corr\": uni,\n           \"eta2_note\": \"axis is NOT combinator identity (eta^2~0.05, s211)\",\n           \"blocks\": blocks_out,\n           \"full\": {\"cv_r2\": full_cv, \"null_mean\": float(null.mean()),\n                    \"null_p95\": float(np.percentile(null, 95)),\n                    \"p_value\": p, \"n_perm\": B}}\n    OUT.write_text(json.dumps(out, indent=2))\n    print(f\"\\nwrote {OUT}\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"
}

### Thinking

 The univariate correlation is striking — `ends_punct` shows an r of 0.877 with the consensus axis, meaning whether a prompt ends with punctuation is almost entirely driving the universal axis. This is a model-free feature that captures something fundamental about how the crystal probes are structured, with some ending cleanly in punctuation and others trailing off mid-expression.

The hierarchical model reveals that the prompt-only block dominates, jumping from 0.547 to 0.813 in cross-validated R², and within that block `ends_punct` is the primary driver. The full model explains 81% of the universal axis variance against a permutation null of −0.045. I notice `ends_space` returned NaN, suggesting it has zero variance across the dataset.

The verdict is clear: the universal axis is fundamentally a "prompt-boundary / continuation-type" gradient, almost entirely determined by whether the prompt ends at punctuation—a model-free property of the text itself. This confirms the axis captures a linguistic property rather than something specific to the model's weights. The distributional features add marginal explanatory power, KL-to-mean is redundant, and combinator identity remains irrelevant. But I should double-check whether the high correlation (r=0.877) is an artifact of how the probe set was constructed—if the crystal probes naturally cluster into prose sentences versus code expressions, then the dominant axis would naturally separate those two groups, which would be real and meaningful rather than spurious.

Let me verify the ends_punct distribution, check whether it correlates with combinator (it shouldn't, given the low η²), and see what happens to the model's performance if I drop that feature entirely. I'll also confirm the baseline numbers match what was reported before.

  "content": "import numpy as np, string\nfrom collections import Counter\nfrom verbum.probes.library import crystal_probes\n\nPUNCT = set(string.punctuation) | {chr(0x201c), chr(0x201d), chr(0x2018),\n    chr(0x2019), chr(0x2014), chr(0x2013), chr(0x2026), chr(0xb7)}\npr = crystal_probes()\nprompts = [p.prompt for p in pr]\nlabs = [p.combinator for p in pr]\nep = np.array([1.0 if (p.rstrip() and p.rstrip()[-1] in PUNCT) else 0.0\n               for p in prompts])\nprint(\"n\", len(prompts), \"ends_punct frac\", round(float(ep.mean()), 3))\nlc = Counter(p.rstrip()[-1] if p.rstrip() else \"\" for p in prompts)\nprint(\"top last-chars:\", lc.most_common(12))\nlab = np.array(labs)\ngrand = ep.mean()\nsstot = ((ep - grand) ** 2).sum() + 1e-30\nssb = sum(len(ep[lab == u]) * (ep[lab == u].mean() - grand) ** 2 for u in set(lab))\nprint(\"eta2(ends_punct~combinator)=\", round(float(ssb / sstot), 3))\nfor u in sorted(set(labs)):\n    print(f\"  {u:5s} ep_rate={ep[lab==u].mean():.2f} n={(lab==u).sum()}\")\nend = [p for p in prompts if p.rstrip() and p.rstrip()[-1] in PUNCT][:5]\nnon = [p for p in prompts if not (p.rstrip() and p.rstrip()[-1] in PUNCT)][:5]\nprint(\"ENDS-PUNCT eg:\")\nfor p in end:\n    print(\"   \", repr(p[-55:]))\nprint(\"NON eg:\")\nfor p in non:\n    print(\"   \", repr(p[-55:]))\n"
}

### Thinking

 I'm seeing that about 28% of probes end with punctuation, and the effect size shows this isn't confounded with the combinator variable—it's distributed uniformly across different combinators, making it orthogonal to the operation labels just like the axis itself.

The semantic distinction is becoming clearer: prompts ending in punctuation (commas in sequences, colons, complete expressions) represent clear continuation points where the next token is often highly predictable, while mid-phrase endings (adjectives, objects, verbs) leave the continuation more open. But I'm noticing something odd with the entropy correlation—the ends_punct end of the axis shows higher entropy despite sequence continuations like "1,2,4,8,16,32,64," being extremely peaked and predictable, which should be low entropy. The sign conventions are making this tricky to interpret.

The real insight is that the universal axis is about 81% explainable, and the dominant signal is a simple textual property—whether the prompt ends in punctuation—accounting for roughly 77% of the variance on its own. This confirms the axis is fundamentally a property of the prompt language itself, not the model or lambda operations. The remaining 19% is harder to pin down, partly because the probe set samples language in a particular way with 28% boundary prompts.

I need to compute the clean R² for ends_punct alone and see what happens when I drop it from the full model to show its dominance. I'll add a quick ablation to the summary script and rerun it.

### Thinking

 A single feature—whether the prompt ends with punctuation—explains 77% of the variance in this universal axis, far outperforming other individual features. When I remove that feature from the full model, the remaining distributional and prompt characteristics still capture 57% of the variance, showing there's meaningful signal in the other features too.

The complete model achieves 81% cross-validated R², confirming this universal axis is genuinely a property of the prompt text itself—something model-free that requires only the prompt string, no weights or forward passes. It's orthogonal to the lambda operations (η²=0.044), so the universal axis really is about textual boundaries and continuation types rather than the combinators themselves.

The remaining 19% appears to be residual prose-shape structure that doesn't reduce to these scalar features. I should note that this finding partly reflects how the probe set was constructed—28% of prompts end at boundaries, creating that bimodality—but this actually sharpens rather than undermines the result: the universal axis across all five model families is capturing a coarse textual property that's fundamental to how all language models work, which is precisely why it's universal and why it's not about the operations.

This connects back to the earlier entropy story from s211—the boundary feature explains much of what entropy and function-fraction were partially capturing. Now I need to finalize the verdict, update the knowledge pages with this concrete finding, and commit the results.

Full model = 0.813 (CV, p=0.005 vs null); without `ends_punct` the remaining features still reach 0.573.

This is the concrete naming: **the dominant universal axis is a property of the prompt text** (needs no weights), orthogonal to the operations (η²=0.044) — exactly why it is universal across 5 families and exactly why it is *not* the lambda structure.

## Assistant

Committed (`155866e`). 

## Universal axis — NAMED

**Question (s211 open lead):** the universal combinator axis (consensus MDS axis-1 of the next-token-prob RDM, |r|=0.95 across 5 families) was only ~30%-named by model-derived proxies. What is the other ~70%?

**Register gate (step 0):** `semantic` — properties of the next-token distribution + model-free prompt-text features. Built-in control: 5-fold **CV-R²** (not in-sample) + a **permutation null**, since fitting ~17 features to 535 points invites overfitting.

**Method:** s211's npz kept only top-64 *indices*, so I re-ran the forward pass saving the **full** distribution (`axis_naming.py`, 8 models / 5 families) → rich distributional features (peakedness, prob-weighted glue mass, KL-to-mean) plus **model-free prompt-text** features.

**Verdict — ✅ NAMED to CV-R²=0.813** (vs permutation null −0.045, p=0.005):

| cumulative block | CV-R² |
|---|---|
| s211 baseline (entropy + function-word frac) | 0.264 |
| + peakedness | 0.442 |
| + glue mass | 0.547 |
| + KL-to-mean | 0.543 *(redundant)* |
| **+ model-free prompt features** | **0.813** |

**The headline:** the dominant component is a single **model-free** feature — **`ends_punct`** (does the prompt end at a punctuation/grammatical boundary) — **CV-R²=0.768 alone**, next-best 0.138. It's orthogonal to the operations (η²=0.044, mirroring the axis's own η²=0.05) and needs no weights at all.

**What it means:** concrete proof of "**property of language, not the model, not the operations**." The most-universal axis across 5 architectures is reproducing a coarse *textual continuation-type / boundary* property of the prompts — which is exactly why all families converge on it and exactly why it isn't the lambda structure. The ~19% residual is the prose-shape common mode CMR removes; KL-to-mean adds nothing once glue-mass is in. Caveat recorded: the magnitude reflects how the probe set samples language (bimodal boundary-vs-mid-phrase prompts).

All written up — knowledge §2b + Open Lead resolved, registry #12e follow-up, state.md headline #2, and a new methodology memory (`universal-axis-is-input-property.md`) — committed `155866e`, chats/ left for you.

**Next leads** (in state.md): the ~19% residual likely isn't scalar-reducible (would need a held-out next-token-dist model) · same-family 2nd shared axis (Qwen×3 CMR residual +0.16) · or pivot to compression carry-overs (#1 gate-vs-value sign-swap PPL, rank-survival across scale).
